ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-04-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00034_d8e22303	Mr President. High house! Let me also briefly comment on a few reports. The transparency database has been discussed for a long time. I think that we should not constantly complain about things, but we must finally let them become effective as instruments. [[Congratulations with representatives of the SPÖ.]] This is what the transparency database lacks. We have examined objectives and objectives, costs and benefits of the transparency database. The result can be summarized as follows: Idea good – implementation modest. The transparency database is not a coherent overall concept, nor is it based on clear objectives that are compatible with each other. A great deal has been projected into the transparency database: first, a purpose of information on the state supply of services, second, a control and abuse prevention purpose in support operations, thirdly, a control purpose in the distribution of services that was intended to be achieved. Finally, the transparency database should contribute to budgetary savings targets in the funding sector as a whole. That was, of course, ambitious. It was much more difficult to implement these ambitious objectives, especially since the federal government can only regulate partial aspects because countries and municipalities are responsible for their own areas. Finally, data protection also played a role in the rights of insight. Therefore, the question to be asked here is: How can and must a database of transparency be established in order to provide maximum transparency and to give citizens maximum benefits in relation to public services? – That’s the goal you finally have to achieve. The transparency database was set up in 2010; in the absence of clear consistency in content and in the absence of complete data, it has not succeeded so far – that is almost eight years ago! – that the goals are fully achieved. A complicated tool was created that was overshadowed by a variety of services and in which there were also weaknesses in the logic of recording. The federal budget has already been mentioned – that is 13,6 million euros. The countries also received €80 million for the introduction of the transparency database and for the establishment of national administrative courts – the latter has now taken place. It was not determined how much the countries consume for the transparency database. As a result of the publication of our report, there were then explanations and intentions that one would try to bring movement into the project. Since 2017, the countries have been committing payments in the field of the environment and energy as part of a pilot project. In principle, Upper Austria registers all payments. Lower Austria, Vorarlberg and the Gemeindebund have also promised their basic willingness. However, countries and municipalities have called for simplification in terms of bureaucracy, and there has been an expert body in the Ministry of Finance since February 2018 that works on models to better record performance in the transparency database. The Court of Auditors shall take part in this information. We have found and also said – and you all know that – that the federal government lacks the competence-based basis on a simple legal basis in order to establish a comprehensive transparency database. There is no obligation on the countries to report the payment data and there is also no obligation to participate in the municipalities. However, the reports at the federal level are also incomplete. The Ministries have the responsibility to register their respective services. On the one hand, a large number of services were summarized, there was no information on the conditions for funding, or there were no services available at all. We have therefore recommended that a declaration of completeness be required by the Ministries so that the database is fully filled. It would also be necessary to make a statement of performance and not just to include payments. Due to the uneven involvement of the services, the objective of the database cannot be fully achieved. For example, funding granted to individual community organisations is not included, including chamber funding, and there is no differentiation between national and EU funds. Benefits were then included, in which it was not fully comprehensible: It will involve the financing of rail infrastructure, the financing of universities, but not the financing of hospitals. So, in view of the lack of an overall concept, there is a lack of logic. One had high expectations with regard to the transparency database, with regard to the control of performance requirements, with regard to the possibility of preventing abuse, with regard to the crossing of funding limits, but the fact is that the resolution agencies, the funding agencies, in practice, seem hardly to have used the transparency database as a control instrument – perhaps because it was not suitable accordingly. In 2015, only 839 government surveys were conducted. In principle, we believe in the idea of the transparency database. Creating transparency is something important, but it is necessary to implement the transparency database fully and in a user-friendly way, thus expanding it into a cross-border database. It is necessary to make the types of services you want to capture logical in terms of content and systematically, and to ensure that decision-makers have sufficient access so that they can also use them for funding. The second report I would like to address concerns the gender aspects of income tax law with a focus on wage tax. This is about equality objectives, which exist in the context of impact orientation. The Court of Auditors too has an equality objective. We take care of how the state deals with its own equality objectives. We have recognised as positive that the Ministry of Finance has set itself an ambitious objective of equality, namely to support the better and more even distribution of labour and unpaid work between women and men through the tax system. It is – and everyone knows that – that the system of individual taxation of course has a very strong and gender-neutral effect, which is positive. However, there are also negative incentives for employment in tax law. These are overtime benefits, the single-earner discount. We have said that there is a need for an overall strategy, because many policy areas have an impact on gender equality and on achieving equal pay for equal work. As far as monopoly management is concerned, we have also worked out the social policy objective in the report. Of course, the objective of granting and awarding trafiques to privileged persons is something of tension with the award of trafiques or tobacco specialist shops to eligible members, which is 28 percent of the cases. We also believe that public procurement is important so that transparency can be maintained in this area. The health aspects and controls on youth protection have been included in the new corporate strategy. We see this positively, because that is an important goal and the reason why this monopoly has been maintained. - Thank you.
